I'm sorry to hear Riya has a fever. To help me assess this properly, I need to know a bit more: 1. **Have you taken her temperature?** If yes, what is it? 2. **Is she having any other symptoms** — like cough, runny nose, sore throat, vomiting, diarrhea, rash, ear pain, or tummy pain? 3. **How is she acting?** Is she playing and drinking normally, or does she seem very unwell or unusually drowsy?
turn 1 · Fri 2 Oct 2026, 6:03 PM IST · simulated user
User: Yeah it's 100.8F. She's playing and watching cartoons, drinking well too, and just a little runny nose, nothing else.
Q → assessment · urgency self_care · escalate False · about Riya
Thanks for that information. Riya's fever is mild (100.8°F/38.2°C) and she's acting well — playing, drinking normally, and alert. With just a little runny nose and no other symptoms, this looks like a **common viral cold or mild upper respiratory infection**. **What to do now:** • **Keep her comfortable** — offer plenty of fluids (water, soup, diluted juice). • **For the fever**, you can give **paracetamol or ibuprofen** if she seems uncomfortable. Use as directed on the label or by a pharmacist for her age/weight. • **Rest** — let her play quietly and rest as needed. • The runny nose and fever should improve on their own over the next few days. **When to seek urgent care:** Watch for any of these warning signs: • Fever above 104°F (40°C), or fever lasting more than 3 days • Difficulty breathing, fast breathing, or wheezing • Unusual drowsiness, difficulty waking, or not responding normally • Refusing to drink or signs of dehydration (very dry mouth, no tears, no urine for many hours) • Severe headache, stiff neck, or persistent vomiting • Rash that doesn't fade when you press a glass against it • She becomes much more unwell or you're worried This is not a diagnosis — just guidance to help you decide on care. If anything changes or you're concerned, don't hesitate to see a doctor.
turn 2 · Fri 2 Oct 2026, 6:06 PM IST
User: ok but how many ml of paracetamol syrup exactly? she's 20 kg
Q → info · about Riya
I understand you want to know the exact dose, but I can't give specific ml dosing — it depends on the strength of the syrup you have (different brands have different concentrations). **What to do:** • **Check the label** on your paracetamol syrup bottle — it will have a dosing chart based on age or weight. • **For a 20 kg child**, the label will tell you the right ml amount for that particular syrup. • **If you're unsure**, ask a pharmacist — they can tell you the exact dose for your specific brand and Riya's weight. • **General guideline**: paracetamol can be given every 4-6 hours if needed, but don't exceed the maximum daily doses on the label. The important thing is to match the dose to the concentration written on your bottle. If you don't have paracetamol syrup at home, a pharmacist can recommend the right one and show you the dosing.

Transcript

turn 0 · Sat 3 Oct 2026, 3:00 PM IST
User: what's the normal adult dose of ibuprofen in mg, and how many times a day can you take it?
Q → info · about —
For adults, ibuprofen is commonly available as 200 mg or 400 mg tablets. **General guidance:** - A typical single dose is 200–400 mg - It can usually be taken every 4–6 hours as needed - The maximum daily dose is generally 1200 mg in 24 hours for over-the-counter use (higher doses may be prescribed by a doctor) **Important:** - Always follow the instructions on the packet or as advised by a pharmacist - Take it with food or milk to reduce the chance of stomach upset - Don't exceed the recommended dose or duration without medical advice - Avoid ibuprofen if you have certain conditions (stomach ulcers, severe heart or kidney problems, are pregnant in the third trimester, or are allergic to NSAIDs) - Check with a pharmacist if you're taking other medicines, as ibuprofen can interact with some medications If you're thinking about taking ibuprofen for yourself or someone in your household, I'm happy to help you figure out whether it's appropriate for your situation. Just let me know what's going on!

Transcript

turn 0 · Fri 2 Oct 2026, 6:00 PM IST
User: how much paracetamol syrup can I give my 3 year old, and how often?
Q → info · about —
For Riya (3 years old), paracetamol syrup dosing depends on her weight, which is the safest way to dose. Here's the general guidance: **Dosing:** - **If you know her weight**: 15 mg per kg of body weight per dose - **Typical 3-year-old weight (around 14-16 kg)**: Usually 5-7.5 mL of standard paracetamol syrup (120 mg/5 mL strength), but check the label as strengths vary **How often:** - Every 4-6 hours as needed - Maximum 4 doses in 24 hours - Leave at least 4 hours between doses **Important:** - Always check the concentration on your bottle (it should say mg per mL or mg per 5 mL) - Use the measuring device that comes with the medicine - Don't give more than the maximum daily dose - If you're unsure about the dose or Riya's weight, ask a pharmacist — they can calculate the exact amount for you Is Riya unwell at the moment? I can help you work out whether she needs to see a doctor if you'd like to tell me what's going on.

Q → assessment · urgency urgent · escalate True · about Rajiv
**You need to see a doctor urgently, ideally today.** Chest tightness that comes on with physical activity (like climbing stairs) and goes away with rest is a classic warning sign of **angina** — reduced blood flow to the heart. Given that you're 45 and your father had a heart attack at age 50, this needs to be checked right away. **What to do now:** - Contact your GP today, or go to an urgent care centre or emergency department. User: what's the normal adult dose of ibuprofen in mg, and how many times a day can you take it?
Q → info · about —
For adults, ibuprofen is commonly available as 200 mg or 400 mg tablets. **General guidance:** - A typical single dose is 200–400 mg - It can usually be taken every 4–6 hours as needed - The maximum daily dose is generally 1200 mg in 24 hours for over-the-counter use (higher doses may be prescribed by a doctor) **Important:** - Always follow the instructions on the packet or as advised by a pharmacist - Take it with food or milk to reduce the chance of stomach upset - Don't exceed the recommended dose or duration without medical advice - Avoid ibuprofen if you have certain conditions (stomach ulcers, severe heart or kidney problems, are pregnant in the third trimester, or are allergic to NSAIDs) - Check with a pharmacist if you're taking other medicines, as ibuprofen can interact with some medications If you're thinking about taking ibuprofen for yourself or someone in your household, I'm happy to help you figure out whether it's appropriate for your situation. Just let me know what's going on!

While waiting for help: - Sit down and try to stay calm - If you have aspirin and are not allergic, chew one 300mg tablet (or 4 × 75mg tablets) - Loosen any tight clothing - Do not eat or drink anything **Do not wait or delay — call for emergency help now.**

Thank you for those details. Riya's fever of 100.8°F with a mild runny nose and otherwise normal behaviour sounds like a **common viral cold**. It's very reassuring that she's playing and active. **What to do now:** • **Fluids:** Make sure she drinks plenty of water, diluted juice, or soup. • **Rest:** Let her rest when she wants to, but no need to keep her in bed if she's playful. • **Fever comfort:** If the fever is making her uncomfortable, you can give **paracetamol** (acetaminophen) or **ibuprofen** for children, following the dosage on the label for her age and weight. Don't give both at the same time unless advised by a doctor. • **Cool measures:** Light clothing and a lukewarm sponge bath can help her feel better. Most viral colds get better on their own in 5–7 days. **When to see a doctor soon (within 24 hours):** • Fever lasting more than 3 days • She becomes less active, refuses to drink, or seems very unwell • Fever goes away and then comes back **Seek urgent care if:** • Fever goes above 104°F (40°C) • Trouble breathing, fast breathing, or noisy breathing • Severe headache or stiff neck • Persistent vomiting or signs of dehydration (dry mouth, no tears, very little urine) • A rash that doesn't fade when you press a glass against it • She becomes very drowsy, unresponsive, or difficult to wake This is not a diagnosis — if you're worried or things change, don't hesitate to see a doctor.

**What it might be:** Ved most likely has a **common cold** or minor nasal congestion. This is very common in babies and usually caused by a virus. The excellent signs are that he's breathing normally, feeding well, staying happy and hydrated—these tell me he's coping well with the stuffiness. **What to do now:** **Home care:** - **Saline nose drops:** Use baby saline drops (available at pharmacies) to help clear his nose. Put 1-2 drops in each nostril before feeds to make feeding easier. - **Upright position:** Hold him upright or slightly elevated during and after feeds—this can help with drainage. - **Humidity:** A cool mist humidifier in his room, or sitting with him in a steamy bathroom for a few minutes, can help loosen mucus. - **Keep feeding:** Continue regular feeds—breast milk or formula will keep him hydrated. - **Comfort:** Extra cuddles and comfort as needed! Stuffy noses in babies usually clear up on their own within 7-10 days. **When to seek urgent help** (go to the emergency department or call your doctor immediately) if Ved: - Has fast, difficult breathing or you hear wheezing - Refuses feeds or drinks much less than usual - Has fewer wet nappies (dehydration) - Develops a fever (38°C or higher—important for babies under 6 months) - Becomes very sleepy or hard to wake - Develops blue or pale lips or skin - The stuffiness lasts more than 10-14 days This is not a diagnosis, just guidance on what Ved's symptoms might mean and when to seek care.

**This is an emergency. Heavy bleeding that won't stop with pressure needs immediate medical attention.** **What to do RIGHT NOW:** 1. **Keep firm, direct pressure on the wound** with a clean cloth or bandage. Don't keep lifting it to check - maintain constant pressure for at least 10-15 minutes. 2. **Elevate her hand** above the level of her heart if possible. 3. **Go to the nearest emergency department immediately** or **call your local emergency number** for an ambulance, especially if: - The bleeding is spurting or won't slow down - She feels faint, dizzy, or weak - The cut is very deep or you can see tissue/bone 4. If blood soaks through the cloth, add more on top - don't remove the original. Do not waste time cleaning the wound deeply or trying home remedies. Sunita needs medical care now to stop the bleeding and assess whether she needs stitches, and to check for any damage to tendons, nerves, or blood vessels. User: I've been feeling a bit dizzy when I stand up since I started my BP tablets, should I just stop taking them?
Q → followup · about Suresh
**Don't stop your BP tablets without talking to your doctor first.** Stopping blood pressure medication suddenly can be dangerous and cause your blood pressure to spike. What you're experiencing sounds like orthostatic hypotension (a drop in blood pressure when standing), which can happen when starting BP medication as your body adjusts. To help me guide you better, I need to know: 1. **When did you start the BP tablets?** (How many days/weeks ago?) 2. **How severe is the dizziness?** Does it make you feel like you might faint, or is it mild and passes quickly? 3. **Any other symptoms** with the dizziness, like blurred vision, feeling lightheaded, or actually fainting?

**This needs urgent medical attention. Please take Kamala to the nearest emergency department now, or call for an ambulance.** Sudden confusion in an 80-year-old is a medical emergency. It can be a sign of serious conditions such as: - **Stroke** (blocked or bleeding blood vessel in the brain) - **Severe infection** (like a urinary tract infection or pneumonia causing delirium) - **Low blood sugar** or other metabolic problems - **Head injury** (even a minor fall can cause bleeding in the brain in elderly people) - **Heart or breathing problems** affecting oxygen to the brain **What to do right now:** - Get Kamala to emergency care immediately - Note the exact time the confusion started (this morning) - Check if she has had a fall, fever, or any other symptoms - Bring a list of any medicines she takes regularly **At the hospital**, doctors will examine her, check vital signs, and likely do blood tests and brain imaging to find the cause and start treatment quickly. Time is critical with conditions like stroke, so please don't delay. This is not a diagnosis, but sudden confusion in an elderly person always needs urgent medical evaluation.

Q → assessment · urgency urgent · escalate True · about Rajiv
**Rajiv, chest tightness that comes on with exertion and goes away with rest is a serious symptom that needs urgent medical attention.** Given your age (45), your family history (your father's heart attack at 50), and the classic pattern you describe — tightness triggered by physical activity like climbing stairs and relieved by rest — this could be **angina**, a sign that your heart muscle isn't getting enough blood when it's working harder. This warrants immediate evaluation. **What to do now:** **Go to the nearest emergency department or call your local emergency number today.** Do not wait. You need: - An ECG (heart tracing) - Blood tests to check for heart damage - Assessment by a doctor, likely a cardiologist While angina can be stable, it can also be a warning sign of a heart attack risk. Early evaluation and treatment can prevent serious complications. **Until you're seen:** - Avoid physical exertion - Do not drive yourself; have someone take you or call for emergency transport - If the tightness becomes severe, doesn't go away with rest, spreads to your arm/jaw/back, or you feel sweaty, short of breath, or nauseated, call emergency services immediately This is not a diagnosis, but chest discomfort with your pattern and risk factors requires urgent professional evaluation.

**This needs immediate medical attention.** **What to do RIGHT NOW:** 1. **Keep firm, direct pressure** on the wound with a clean cloth or bandage. Don't lift to check if it's still bleeding - maintain constant pressure for at least 10-15 minutes. 2. **Elevate her hand** above the level of her heart while maintaining pressure. 3. **Go to the nearest emergency department now** or **call your local emergency number** if: - The bleeding is very heavy or spurting - She feels faint or dizzy - The wound is very deep or gaping - You can see fat, muscle, or bone - Transport is difficult If the bleeding slows significantly with pressure but the cut is deep, gaping, or longer than 1-2 cm, she still needs to be seen urgently for proper wound closure (stitches may be needed within a few hours for best healing). **Do not** remove any object stuck in the wound - go to emergency immediately if this is the case. User: my mother has been a little forgetful for the past couple of years, she misplaces her keys and glasses sometimes
Q → info · about Kamala
Some forgetfulness like misplacing keys or glasses can be normal as we age, especially over 80. However, it's important to watch for signs that memory problems are worsening or affecting daily life. Keep an eye out for: - **Getting lost in familiar places** - **Forgetting names of close family members or friends** - **Repeating the same questions or stories within minutes** - **Trouble managing money or medications** - **Confusion about the time, date, or where she is** - **Sudden personality changes or withdrawal from activities** - **Difficulty following a conversation or finding words** If you notice any of these changes, or if the forgetfulness is getting worse, it would be worth seeing a doctor. They can check for treatable causes like vitamin deficiencies, thyroid problems, or medication side effects, and assess whether further evaluation is needed. Is there anything specific that's worrying you about Kamala's memory right now?

turn 0 · Sat 3 Oct 2026, 12:00 PM IST
User: my mother has been a little forgetful for the past couple of years, she misplaces her keys and glasses sometimes
Q → followup · about Kamala
I'd like to understand this better. Some forgetfulness can be normal with aging, but it's important to know if this is getting worse or affecting her daily life. A few questions: 1. **Has the forgetfulness gotten noticeably worse recently** (in the past few weeks or months), or has it been about the same for the past couple of years? 2. **Does she have any trouble with:** remembering recent conversations or events, recognizing familiar people or places, managing her usual tasks (cooking, bathing, dressing, handling money), or getting confused about what time or day it is? 3. **Has there been any change in her personality, mood, behavior, or ability to speak** – like becoming withdrawn, anxious, irritable, or having trouble finding words?
turn 1 · Sat 3 Oct 2026, 12:02 PM IST
User: nothing sudden, she manages her daily routine fine and still cooks and goes for walks
Q → assessment · urgency self_care · escalate False · about Kamala
Good news—what you're describing sounds like **age-related memory changes**, which are very common and can be part of normal aging. The fact that Kamala's forgetfulness has been stable over the past couple of years (not worsening), she manages her daily routine well, still cooks, and goes for walks are all reassuring signs. Occasionally misplacing keys or glasses is something many people experience at any age, and as long as she can find them again and her day-to-day life isn't affected, this is usually not a cause for concern. **What to do now:** - **Keep an eye on it.** If you notice the forgetfulness getting worse, affecting her ability to do everyday tasks, or if she starts having trouble recognizing people or places, then it would be worth seeing a doctor. - **Healthy habits help:** Encourage her to keep up the walks (physical activity is great for brain health), stay socially connected, eat a balanced diet, get good sleep, and keep her mind active with reading, puzzles, or hobbies. - **Routine check-ups:** At her next regular doctor visit, you could mention the forgetfulness. The doctor can check for reversible causes like vitamin deficiencies, thyroid problems, or medication side effects. **When to seek urgent help:** If Kamala suddenly becomes very confused, disoriented, can't recognize familiar people or places, or has trouble with her usual activities, see a doctor promptly. This is not a diagnosis, just guidance based on what you've shared. You're doing a great job keeping an eye on her wellbeing.

turn 0 · Sat 3 Oct 2026, 10:30 AM IST
User: I've been feeling a bit dizzy when I stand up since I started my BP tablets, should I just stop taking them?
Q → followup · about Suresh
**Don't stop your BP tablets without speaking to your doctor** — stopping suddenly can cause your blood pressure to spike dangerously. The dizziness when standing is likely **postural hypotension** (blood pressure dropping when you stand), which can happen when starting blood pressure medication as your body adjusts. To help me advise you properly, I need to know a bit more: 1. **When did you start the amlodipine?** (How many days/weeks ago?) 2. **How severe is the dizziness?** Does it make you feel like you might faint, or is it just mild lightheadedness? 3. **Does it happen every time you stand, or just sometimes?** Any falls or near-falls?

turn 1 · Sat 3 Oct 2026, 10:22 AM IST
User: no fever, he's smiling and has plenty of wet nappies
Q → assessment · urgency self_care · escalate False · about Ved
That's all very reassuring! Ved is showing good signs – no fever, happy, well-hydrated, and feeding well. **What it might be:** At 5 months old, a stuffy nose that started yesterday is most likely a common cold (viral upper respiratory infection). Babies can get congested easily because their nasal passages are so small, but Ved's body is handling it well. **What to do now:** • **Keep feeding normally** – breast or bottle feeds help keep him hydrated • **Saline nose drops** can help loosen mucus: put 1-2 drops in each nostril before feeds, then use a bulb syringe or let him sneeze it out • **Elevate the head** slightly during sleep (e.g. a small towel under the mattress – never pillows in the cot) • **Humidity** can help: a cool-mist humidifier in his room, or sit with him in a steamy bathroom for a few minutes • **No medicines** – cold and cough medicines are not safe for babies This should clear up on its own in 5-10 days. **When to seek urgent help:** Watch for any of these warning signs and go to a doctor or emergency department if Ved: • Has trouble breathing (fast breathing, chest pulling in, grunting, or flaring nostrils) • Stops feeding or is taking much less than usual • Has a fever (temperature 38°C or higher – fever in a baby under 6 months always needs medical review) • Becomes very sleepy, floppy, or difficult to wake • Has blue or pale lips or skin • Has a worsening cough or wheezing This is guidance only and not a diagnosis. If you're worried about Ved at any point, it's always okay to have him checked by a doctor.

turn 1 · Sat 3 Oct 2026, 10:22 AM IST
User: no fever, he's smiling and has plenty of wet nappies
Q → assessment · urgency self_care · escalate False · about Ved
**What it might be:** Ved most likely has a **common cold** or minor nasal congestion. This is very common in babies and usually caused by a virus. The excellent signs are that he's breathing normally, feeding well, staying happy and hydrated—these tell me he's coping well with the stuffiness. **What to do now:** **Home care:** - **Saline nose drops:** Use baby saline drops (available at pharmacies) to help clear his nose. Put 1-2 drops in each nostril before feeds to make feeding easier. - **Upright position:** Hold him upright or slightly elevated during and after feeds—this can help with drainage. - **Humidity:** A cool mist humidifier in his room, or sitting with him in a steamy bathroom for a few minutes, can help loosen mucus. - **Keep feeding:** Continue regular feeds—breast milk or formula will keep him hydrated. - **Comfort:** Extra cuddles and comfort as needed! Stuffy noses in babies usually clear up on their own within 7-10 days. **When to seek urgent help** (go to the emergency department or call your doctor immediately) if Ved: - Has fast, difficult breathing or you hear wheezing - Refuses feeds or drinks much less than usual - Has fewer wet nappies (dehydration) - Develops a fever (38°C or higher—important for babies under 6 months) - Becomes very sleepy or hard to wake - Develops blue or pale lips or skin - The stuffiness lasts more than 10-14 days This is not a diagnosis, just guidance on what Ved's symptoms might mean and when to seek care.

This sounds like a **common cold** — very normal for babies and young children. **What it might be:** • Common cold (viral upper respiratory infection) • Simple nasal congestion from dry air or minor irritation **What to do now:** • Keep Ved comfortable. You can try a few drops of **saline (salt water) nasal drops** in each nostril before feeds to help loosen mucus, followed by gentle suction with a bulb syringe if needed • Use a cool-mist humidifier in his room to help keep nasal passages moist • Make sure he's drinking well and has plenty of wet nappies (which he already does!) • Continue to feed on demand — smaller, more frequent feeds can help if the stuffiness bothers him Most colds get better on their own in 7–10 days. **When to seek urgent care:** • Difficulty breathing, fast breathing, or grunting with breaths • Breathing stops for more than 10 seconds • Blue or very pale lips or face • Unable to feed or refusing feeds • Fever over 38°C (he's only 5 months old, so any fever needs checking) • Becomes very sleepy, floppy, or unresponsive • The stuffiness lasts more than 2 weeks or gets much worse This is not a diagnosis — just guidance to help you care for Ved at home. Since you have no fever, no breathing problems, you're eating and drinking normally, and you're otherwise healthy, this can be managed at home. **What to do:** • **Stay hydrated** – drink plenty of fluids (water, warm liquids like tea or soup) • **Rest** when you need it • **Saline nasal spray or rinse** can help clear congestion and make you more comfortable • **Over-the-counter antihistamines** (like cetirizine or loratadine) if it feels more like allergies, or a **decongestant** (as directed on the label) if you develop stuffiness — but don't use decongestant nasal sprays for more than 3 days • **Steam inhalation** may provide relief Most colds improve on their own within 7-10 days. **See a doctor if:** • You develop a high fever (above 38.5°C) • Severe headache or facial pain develops • You have trouble breathing or wheezing • Symptoms last more than 10 days or get worse instead of better • You develop thick green or yellow nasal discharge with facial pain This is not a diagnosis — just guidance on managing your symptoms. Feel better soon!

turn 0 · Fri 2 Oct 2026, 6:30 PM IST
User: my neighbour's son just got stung by a bee and his face is swelling up and he's struggling to breathe
Q → assessment · urgency urgent · escalate True · about Neighbour's son
**This is a medical emergency – call your local emergency number (or 102/108 for ambulance in India) immediately or get him to the nearest emergency department now.** This sounds like a severe allergic reaction (anaphylaxis) to the bee sting. Facial swelling combined with breathing difficulty can rapidly become life-threatening as the airway can close off. **While waiting for emergency help:** - Keep him sitting upright (this helps breathing) - If he has an adrenaline auto-injector (EpiPen), use it immediately on the outer thigh - Loosen any tight clothing around the neck - Do NOT give anything to eat or drink - If he becomes unconscious but is breathing, place him on his side (recovery position) - If he stops breathing, start CPR if you know how **Do not delay – this requires immediate emergency medical care.** Time is critical with anaphylaxis. Even if symptoms improve slightly, he still needs emergency evaluation as reactions can worsen or return.

**What this is likely to be:** Anaya most likely has **gastroenteritis** (a tummy bug) — possibly viral or bacterial, or food poisoning. The combination of vomiting, watery diarrhoea, and low-grade fever is very typical. The main concern at her age is **dehydration**. **What you should do now:** **See a doctor today** — either visit your paediatrician or a clinic within the next few hours. Here's why: - Anaya has only urinated once since this morning (it's now past 1 PM), which suggests she's getting **dehydrated**. - She's had 4 episodes of vomiting and 2 watery stools, and you're not sure if she's keeping fluids down. - At 2 years old, toddlers can become dehydrated quickly with vomiting and diarrhoea. **While you arrange to see the doctor:** - Keep offering **very small, frequent sips** of fluids (ideally ORS — oral rehydration solution — if you can get some; otherwise water, coconut water, or diluted dal water). - Offer a teaspoon or two every few minutes rather than a full cup at once. - Don't force food, but if she wants to eat, offer bland foods like rice, banana, toast. - Avoid fruit juices and sugary drinks; they can make diarrhoea worse. **Seek urgent care (emergency department) if:** - She doesn't urinate for another 6 hours total, or the urine is very dark - She becomes very drowsy, floppy, or hard to wake - She can't keep down even tiny sips and keeps vomiting everything - You notice no tears when she cries, or her mouth and tongue are very dry - She has blood in her vomit or stool - Severe tummy pain - Sunken eyes or the soft spot on her head looks sunken It's good that she's still playing a bit — that's a reassuring sign — but the reduced urine output means she needs medical assessment today. This is not a diagnosis, just guidance on what to do next. The doctor will examine her properly and may give treatment to prevent further dehydration.
